# Frozen peas

<section class="hmi-quick-read" id="frozen-peas-quick-read" data-hmi-quick-read aria-labelledby="frozen-peas-quick-read-label"><div class="hmi-quick-read-label" id="frozen-peas-quick-read-label">Quick read</div>

Frozen peas in the HMI library has 37 reported results for lead, cadmium, total arsenic, total mercury, nickel, chromium, aluminium, uranium, Co, Cu, Fe, Mn, Zn, V, B, Ba, Ca, Mg and Mo from 4 sources.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · ## Frozen peas overview

<a id="which-contamination-concerns-are-supported-for-this-ingredient-and-its-defined-forms"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-005"></a>

<a id="hmi-ing-006"></a>
<a id="which-evidence-gaps-most-limit-those-decisions"></a>

Frozen peas in the HMI library has 37 reported results for lead, cadmium, total arsenic, total mercury, nickel, chromium, aluminium, uranium, Co, Cu, Fe, Mn, Zn, V, B, Ba, Ca, Mg and Mo from 4 sources.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">GEMS/Food Contaminants database heavy-metal exports</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">GEMS/Food Contamination Monitoring and Assessment Programme</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020|2]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">U.S. Food and Drug Administration · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">FY2018-FY2020 TDS Elements Analytical Results</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">FDA Total Diet Study</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables|3]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Tokay F · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables">Determination of Some Elements in Canned and Frozen Vegetables Sold in Balıkesir (Türkiye) Markets and Nutritional Assessment</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Adıyaman University Journal of Science (ADYU J SCI) 12(2), 230-243</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium|4]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Spungen JH, et al. · 2024</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Infants&#39; and young children&#39;s dietary exposures to lead and cadmium: FDA total diet study 2018-2020</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Food Additives &amp; Contaminants: Part A</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Read source →</a></span></span></sup> WHO GEMS/Food submissions reported lead in frozen peas, as sold from 90 results in 2 reporting regions, with a subgroup median of 3.45 µg/kg.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">GEMS/Food Contaminants database heavy-metal exports</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">GEMS/Food Contamination Monitoring and Assessment Programme</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">Read source →</a></span></span></sup> In the US Total Diet Study, frozen peas, cooked had a median lead result below 4 µg/kg across 3 composites from 2018–2020.<sup class="cite">[[sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020|2]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">U.S. Food and Drug Administration · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">FY2018-FY2020 TDS Elements Analytical Results</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">FDA Total Diet Study</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Spungen et al. 2024 reported cadmium at 1.7 µg/kg (mean) in frozen peas, cooked from all samples.<sup class="cite">[[sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium|4]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Spungen JH, et al. · 2024</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Infants&#39; and young children&#39;s dietary exposures to lead and cadmium: FDA total diet study 2018-2020</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Food Additives &amp; Contaminants: Part A</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

For a buyer the practical points are the form bought, the basis a result is reported on, where the frozen peas came from and the laboratory’s reporting limit. Testing finds contamination but does not remove it.



<a id="ingredient-identity-and-scope"></a>
## What counts as frozen peas

<a id="hmi-ing-001"></a>
<a id="what-commodity-biological-species-edible-part-and-commercial-forms-does-this-page-cover"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-002"></a>
<a id="which-processing-states-and-derivatives-are-distinct-materials-rather-than-synonyms"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-003"></a>
<a id="what-evidence-can-be-transferred-between-those-materials-and-what-requires-a-separate-answer"></a>

This page covers frozen peas in 3 forms kept separate in every comparison: frozen peas, frozen (16 results), frozen peas, as sold (12 results) and frozen peas, cooked (9 results).<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-journal">GEMS/Food Contamination Monitoring and Assessment Programme</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020|2]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">U.S. Food and Drug Administration · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">FY2018-FY2020 TDS Elements Analytical Results</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite">[[sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables|3]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Tokay F · 2022</span><a Nutritional Assessment</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Adıyaman University Journal of Science (ADYU J SCI) 12(2), 230-243</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium|4]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Spungen JH, et al. · 2024</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Infants&#39; and young children&#39;s dietary exposures to lead and cadmium: FDA total diet study 2018-2020</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Food Additives &amp; Contaminants: Part A</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Results stay on the basis each source reports: as sold or fresh weight, dry weight, or unstated. Dry-weight, as-sold and unstated results are never pooled, and no moisture conversion is applied.

<div class="hmi-analyte-explorer" id="hmi-occurrence-frozen-peas" data-analyte-explorer data-compact-intro="true" data-source-summaries="true"><details class="hmi-drawer-disclosure" data-explorer-context><summary data-open-context aria-haspopup="dialog" aria-controls="hmi-occurrence-frozen-peas-drawer">Comparison methods and scope</summary><div data-explorer-context-content>

<p>Each row is one statistic a source reports for one form of frozen peas, group and metal; percentiles, ranges and maxima reported for the same samples as a mean or median are shown on that row. Different forms and preparation states are kept apart, and dry-weight results never share a reference with as-sold or fresh-weight results. Ratings place a result against an HMI reference built from monitoring results and ordinary-setting medians for the same form and basis. Same-study contrasts compare a contaminated or treated site with its control, targeted with random sampling, a prepared form with the unprocessed one, or one group of a study with the median of its other groups. Held rows failed a check against their own source and are shown but not compared.</p>

Counts describe evidence units, not independent studies or certainty.</p><div class="hmi-drawer-table"><table><thead><tr><th scope="col">Contributing family</th><th scope="col">Reference units</th><th scope="col">Weight</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Tokay 2022</td><td>1</td><td>100%</td></tr></tbody></table></div><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Methods</p><p>HMI pools subgroup medians for frozen peas, frozen on a as sold, as eaten or fresh weight basis: monitoring results summarized as one median per reporting region, sampling type and collection year, total diet composites and printed sample values as single observations, and printed subgroup medians from ordinary farms or markets. Each sampling family has equal total weight. Non-detects stay as zero-to-limit intervals, so quartiles carry lower and upper bounds. Means, ranges and maxima never enter as observations. Other forms, other bases, targeted sampling and results from mining, industrial, wastewater-affected, experimental, fertiliser or manufacturer settings are kept out of this reference.</p><p>Inverse weighted empirical CDF; midpoint at exact cumulative-weight boundaries. At least four reference units and a definitely positive Q1 to Q3 spread are required. This is a display eligibility rule, not statistical validation.</p><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Limitations</p><p>Tokay 2022 is the only compatible sampling family for frozen peas, frozen on this basis in the completed frozen peas ledger, so this single-family reference cannot represent other markets. Reporting regions and purchase markets are not growing areas. Non-detects with differing limits widen the bounds. A position is descriptive concentration, not a safety or compliance judgment.</p><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Admitted source families</p><ul><li><a href="../sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables" class="internal" data-slug="sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables" data-no-cite-meta>Tokay 2022</a></li></ul><details class="hmi-drawer-disclosure"><summary>Reference version and interpretation</summary><p>Frozen evidence selection: 2026-10-22. data-slug="sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables">Tokay 2022</a></p><p>Frozen peas, frozen · Lead (Pb) · 3 samples.</p><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Selected finding and reference</p><div class="hmi-selected-result" data-selected-finding><strong class="hmi-finding">1,300 µg/kg (sample value ± 100 SD)</strong><span class="hmi-row-note">sample value.</span><p>3 samples. Basis: As sold, as eaten or fresh weight.</p><p>No censoring reported</p></div><div class="hmi-selected-comparison" data-selected-comparison><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">HMI baseline position</p><p>Limited data · HMI frozen peas Pb reference · Frozen peas, frozen. sample value as reported: 1300 µg/kg. HMI median 1300; Q1 1300; Q3 1300 µg/kg. 1 family. Too few reference units.</p><p>Only 1 reference unit is available; at least four are required by this descriptive display rule.</p><p>This finding’s sampling family contributes to the fixed reference.</p><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Sensitivity to weighting</p><p>Equal weight per subgroup: Limited data. The position remains unresolved under this alternative. This check does not address all sampling, rounding or analytical uncertainty.</p><div data-baseline-reference="hmi-pooled-reference-v2:frozen-peas-d81475d8009d:c271dc8d6250"><a href="#hmi-reference-hmi-pooled-reference-v2:frozen-peas-d81475d8009d:c271dc8d6250">Reference, contributing evidence and methods</a></div></div><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Collection period</p><p data-timeline-scope>Collection date unreported</p><div data-selected-timeline><p>No year-by-year sample register is reconstructed from this summary.</p></div><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Geographic scope</p><p data-regional-scope>retail sample 1, local supermarkets, Balikesir</p><div data-selected-regional><p>Product category within one study.</p></div><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Sampling, analysis and reporting limits</p><p>Microwave HNO3 digestion (CEM Mars 5) of about 1.0 g homogenized sample; ICP-OES; three independent digestions per sample.</p><p>No censoring reported</p><p>Mean ± SD of three independent digestions of one retail product. Text gives 1.34 ± 0.10 mg/kg. Only Pb result above LOD; high for peas, single product.</p><p class="hmi-drawer-section-title" role="heading" aria-level="3">Shared samples and study overlap</p><p>Subgroup rows from the same source share its laboratory and sampling frame; they are not independent studies.</p><p>Each table row describes a food/analyte result. Repeated rows do not add independent samples or datasets. No cross-study trend is calculated in this comparison mode.</p><details class="hmi-drawer-disclosure"><summary>Reported values, conversion and source locator</summary><div class="hmi-drawer-table"><table><thead><tr><th scope="col">Source statistic</th><th scope="col">Original result</th><th scope="col">Displayed unit conversion</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>sample_value</td><td>1.3±0.1 mg/kg</td><td>as recorded in the findings store</td></tr></tbody></table></div><p>Table 3, p. 239.</p><p>No moisture, cooking-yield or species conversion is applied to the original finding above. Any basis conversion used for its HMI comparison is stated separately with the numerical target.</p></details><p><a href="https://doi.org/10.37094/adyujsci.1181786">Open the primary publication</a> · <a href="../sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables" class="internal" data-slug="sources/tokay2022-balikesir-canned-frozen-vegetables">HMI source record</a></p></div></div></div><p class="hmi-explorer-empty" data-explorer-empty hidden>No findings match these filters. <a id="sources-and-contamination-pathways"></a>
## How metals reach frozen peas

<a id="hmi-ing-010"></a>
<a id="what-evidence-supports-root-uptake-surface-deposition-feed-transfer-or-other-biological-pathways"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-011"></a>
<a id="what-contamination-can-arise-from-agricultural-inputs-harvesting-processing-equipment-or-packaging"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-012"></a>
<a id="which-source-attribution-claims-are-measured-modelled-inferred-or-unresolved"></a>

Peas (Pisum sativum L.) take up cadmium, lead, nickel, and chromium from soil through root absorption, with subsequent translocation to stems and leaves. Five studies involving experimentally contaminated substrates document these pathways in Pisum sativum.

Cadmium enters pea roots from soil and translocates to leaves in a dose-dependent manner. A field experiment in Faisalabad, Pakistan, applied cadmium as CdCl2 at soil concentrations of 120 and 240 mg/kg and measured Cd accumulation in roots and leaves by ICP-MS. Leaf Cd content increased by approximately 58% above control at 120 mg/kg soil Cd and by approximately 78% above control at 240 mg/kg soil Cd. Root Cd increased by approximately 64% and 81% at the same doses. At both doses, Cd stress significantly reduced leaf concentrations of nitrogen (31-142% below control), iron (51-135%), zinc (37-123%), potassium (31-80%), and magnesium (25-86%), indicating competitive interference with essential micronutrient uptake.<sup class="cite">[[sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea|5]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Hashmat S et al. · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea">Microbial intervention by Acinetobacter schindleri SR-5-1 alleviates cadmium toxicity in pea cultivation</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Plant Signaling and Behavior</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea">Read source →</a></span></span></sup> A hydroponic study confirmed cadmium uptake by pea roots and translocation to leaves: at 9 uM CdCl2 (approximately 1 mg/L) applied for 12 days, roots accumulated approximately 120 mg/kg Cd; leaf Cd was lower. Silica nanoparticle co-treatment reduced leaf Cd by 67.88% and root Cd by 22.24% relative to Cd-only plants, indicating that root absorption can be partially suppressed by silica binding.<sup class="cite">[[sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings|6]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Yang X et al. · 2025</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings">Silica nanoparticles alleviate cadmium toxicity to Pisum sativum L. seedling growth by remodeling carbon-nitrogen metabolism</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Frontiers in Plant Science</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Chromium applied to soil also enters pea roots and accumulates in shoot tissue. A pot experiment in Faisalabad applied 100 uM Cr (approximately 5.2 mg/L) as CrCl3 and measured Cr in soil, shoot, and grain by atomic absorption spectrometry. Soil, shoot, and grain Cr increased 69.4% (Supreme variety) and 44.5% (Meteor variety) above control. Chromium stress also disrupted ionic balance: shoot K+ decreased 39.4-39.7% and shoot Ca2+ decreased 33.1-39.8% relative to control, while shoot Na+ increased 60.9-67.1%.<sup class="cite">[[sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea|7]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Faryad H et al. · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea">Mitigating chromium stress on pea (Pisum sativum L.) by foliar application of trehalose</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">BMC Plant Biology</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Pea plants respond to nickel and lead through antioxidant defense and metal chelation gene pathways. A greenhouse study in Rize, Turkiye, exposed three forage pea cultivars (Pisum sativum ssp. arvense) to nickel sulphate (20 and 40 mg/L) and lead acetate trihydrate (20 and 40 mg/L). Expression of APX (ascorbate peroxidase), CAT (catalase), MT (metallothionein), and PCS (phytochelatin synthase) genes was measured by qRT-PCR. At 40 mg/L lead acetate trihydrate, APX expression in the Kirazli cultivar reached approximately 43-fold above control. MT expression in Kurtbey reached approximately 8.7-fold above control at 20 mg/L lead. These responses indicate the plant is sequestering metal ions intracellularly via phytochelatins and scavenging reactive oxygen species. Lead acetate trihydrate at 20 mg/L reduced mean plant height from 46.88 cm to 39.66 cm (p less than 0.05), confirming phytotoxicity at the applied dose. ANOVA found significant APX gene expression differences attributable to heavy metal treatment (p = 0.0472).<sup class="cite">[[sources/mesci2025-nickel-lead-stress-genes-forage-pea|8]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Mesci S, Catal MI · 2025</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/mesci2025-nickel-lead-stress-genes-forage-pea">Effects of nickel sulphate and lead acetate trihydrate on heavy metal stress-related gene activities in forage pea (Pisum sativum ssp. arvense L.) in Turkiye</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Frontiers in Plant Science</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/mesci2025-nickel-lead-stress-genes-forage-pea">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Copper enters pea seedling roots and distributes to stems and leaves. In a greenhouse study measuring copper in soil-cultivated pea seedling tissues, root Cu ranged from 0.392 to 0.552 mg/kg without added Cu and increased to 0.462 to 0.649 mg/kg with 10 mg/L Cu addition in the substrate. Stem and leaf Cu concentrations were similarly elevated by Cu addition. This study used a pesticide co-exposure design; the Cu tissue concentrations are reported from ICP-MS on fresh tissue samples.<sup class="cite">[[sources/xiong2026-copper-tolfenpyrad-pea-seedlings|9]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Xiong W et al. · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/xiong2026-copper-tolfenpyrad-pea-seedlings">Effect of copper and cultivation condition on the transport, metabolism, and potential risk of tolfenpyrad in pea seedlings (Pisum sativum Linn.)</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">iScience</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/xiong2026-copper-tolfenpyrad-pea-seedlings">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

All five studies used experimentally elevated metal concentrations (soil Cd 120-240 mg/kg, Cr 100 uM, Ni and Pb 20-40 mg/L solution, Cu 10 mg/L addition). These are stress physiology experiments, not food surveys. The concentration levels applied substantially exceed typical agricultural soil background levels for most of these metals. The experiments document that the uptake pathway is active and that metals do translocate from roots to shoot tissue; they do not provide finished-food concentrations for frozen peas sold commercially.



<a id="ranges-by-source-region-and-variety"></a>
## Which forms and regions measured lower

<a id="hmi-ing-013"></a>
<a id="how-do-concentrations-vary-by-origin-growing-conditions-variety-and-harvest-period"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-014"></a>
<a id="what-evidence-distinguishes-within-lot-between-lot-supplier-and-production-system-variation"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-015"></a>
<a id="which-comparisons-support-a-sourcing-decision-and-where-would-geographic-or-supplier-generalization-be-unjustified"></a>

The HMI reference for lead in frozen peas, cooked pools 4 subgroup units from 2 sampling families, with a median of below 4 µg/kg and a middle half spanning below 4 (lower quartile) and below 4 µg/kg (upper quartile).<sup class="cite">[[sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020|2]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">U.S. Food and Drug Administration · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">FY2018-FY2020 TDS Elements Analytical Results</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">FDA Total Diet Study</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">Read source →</a></span></span></sup><sup class="cite">[[sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium|4]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Spungen JH, et al. · 2024</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Infants&#39; and young children&#39;s dietary exposures to lead and cadmium: FDA total diet study 2018-2020</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Food Additives &amp; Contaminants: Part A</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/spungen2024-fda-tds-infant-lead-cadmium">Read source →</a></span></span></sup> For lead in frozen peas, as sold, the reference median was below 7 µg/kg, with a middle half from below 3.75 to below 7 µg/kg across 22 units.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">GEMS/Food Contaminants database heavy-metal exports</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">GEMS/Food Contamination Monitoring and Assessment Programme</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">Read source →</a></span></span></sup> For cadmium in frozen peas, as sold, the reference median was between 1.25 and 2 µg/kg, with a middle half from between 0.75 and 2 to between 2.5 and 3.5 µg/kg across 16 units.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">GEMS/Food Contaminants database heavy-metal exports</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">GEMS/Food Contamination Monitoring and Assessment Programme</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

<a id="mitigation-options"></a>
## Mitigation for frozen peas

<a id="hmi-ing-019"></a>
<a id="which-prevention-mitigation-or-remediation-measures-have-been-evaluated-for-this-ingredient"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-020"></a>
<a id="under-what-conditions-at-which-stage-and-against-which-comparator-do-they-work-or-fail"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-021"></a>
<a id="what-evidence-supports-field-or-commercial-implementation-and-ongoing-verification"></a>

Lot testing against the applicable limit is the check that can be verified.

Two categories of pre-harvest intervention have been evaluated in Pisum sativum experiments using experimentally elevated metal concentrations. Both are pathway-stage interventions; neither has been validated under commercial growing conditions.

Foliar application of trehalose reduced chromium accumulation in pea shoot and grain tissue. In a pot experiment applying 100 uM Cr (approximately 5.2 mg/L) to soil, trehalose sprayed at 10 mM concentration reduced shoot Cr by 31.6% in the Supreme variety and 22.3% in the Meteor variety relative to untreated Cr-stressed plants. Trehalose at 5 mM produced smaller reductions (7-18% in shoots). The intervention also partially restored the K+ and Ca2+ imbalances caused by Cr stress, with shoot K+ increasing 42.8-47.3% above the Cr-stressed control after 10 mM trehalose.<sup class="cite">[[sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea|7]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Faryad H et al. · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea">Mitigating chromium stress on pea (Pisum sativum L.) by foliar application of trehalose</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">BMC Plant Biology</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/faryad2026-chromium-stress-trehalose-pea">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Rhizosphere bacterial inoculation reduced cadmium accumulation in pea leaves and roots. In a field experiment in Faisalabad applying CdCl2 at 120 and 240 mg/kg soil, inoculation with Acinetobacter schindleri SR-5-1 reduced leaf Cd content by approximately 7.21% at 120 mg/kg and 10.85% at 240 mg/kg, relative to non-inoculated stressed plants. The reductions are partial; at 240 mg/kg soil Cd, inoculated plants still accumulated substantially more Cd than control plants grown in uncontaminated soil.<sup class="cite">[[sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea|5]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Hashmat S et al. · 2026</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea">Microbial intervention by Acinetobacter schindleri SR-5-1 alleviates cadmium toxicity in pea cultivation</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Plant Signaling and Behavior</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/hashmat2026-acinetobacter-cadmium-pea">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

Silica nanoparticle co-treatment in a hydroponic study reduced leaf cadmium by 67.88% and root cadmium by 22.24% at 9 uM CdCl2. The mechanism appears to involve silica binding at the root surface, reducing initial Cd absorption before shoot translocation occurs.<sup class="cite">[[sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings|6]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">Yang X et al. · 2025</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings">Silica nanoparticles alleviate cadmium toxicity to Pisum sativum L. seedling growth by remodeling carbon-nitrogen metabolism</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span class="cite-card-journal">Frontiers in Plant Science</span><a class="cite-card-review" href="../sources/yang2025-silica-nanoparticles-cadmium-pea-seedlings">Read source →</a></span></span></sup>

No intervention study measured finished-food metal concentrations in commercially produced or frozen peas. The experiments document biologically plausible reduction mechanisms; regulatory or commercial application remains unvalidated.



<a id="trade-offs-and-failure-modes"></a>
## Trade-offs and limits of controls

<a id="hmi-ing-022"></a>
<a id="can-an-intervention-increase-another-contaminant-or-redistribute-contamination-to-a-by-product"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-023"></a>

<a id="hmi-ing-024"></a>
<a id="what-costs-constraints-adverse-effects-or-failure-modes-are-documented-and-which-are-unknown"></a>

Metals do not move together, so a lot that passes a lead limit says little about cadmium, arsenic or nickel unless those are tested as well.

Results depend on the method and its reporting limit. Reporting limits for lead ranged from 2 µg/kg (WHO GEMS/Food) to 3,000 µg/kg (WHO GEMS/Food), so a non-detect from a less sensitive method says much less.<sup →</a></span></span></sup> Reporting limits for cadmium ranged from 1 µg/kg (WHO GEMS/Food) to 1,000 µg/kg (WHO GEMS/Food), so a non-detect from a less sensitive method says much less.<sup ## Evidence review and remaining gaps

<a id="hmi-ing-034"></a>
<a id="which-primary-regulatory-instruments-apply-to-this-exact-ingredient-form-species-market-and-date"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-035"></a>
<a id="what-searches-eligibility-decisions-source-families-and-appraisal-underpin-the-answers-and-certainty-judgments"></a>
<a id="hmi-ing-036"></a>
<a id="what-remains-unresolved-when-was-each-answer-reviewed-and-what-changed-in-this-version"></a>

Every source record that names frozen peas was decided on its content: 4 occurrence sources extracted, 9 records in all. Five additional sources document cadmium, chromium, nickel, lead, and copper uptake pathways in Pisum sativum under experimentally elevated metal concentrations; these are classified as plant-uptake pathway context and are not entered into the occurrence table. The occurrence table renders every extracted result; held values remain visible with their reason.

References identify the sources used in these answers. The Sources index preserves additional discovery links; inclusion there does not mean a source has been reviewed for this assessment. Corrections, completed searches, new direct evidence and changes in requirements should reopen the relevant answer.

## Frozen peas FAQs

<details class="qa" data-hmi-faq="1" id="what-heavy-metals-are-found-in-frozen-peas" data-anchor="what-heavy-metals-are-found-in-frozen-peas" data-question="What heavy metals are found in frozen peas?">

The library reports lead, cadmium, total arsenic, total mercury, nickel, chromium, aluminium, uranium, Co, Cu, Fe, Mn, Zn, V, B, Ba, Ca, Mg and Mo in frozen peas, most often lead, cadmium and total arsenic.<sup class="cite">[[sources/who-gemsfood-heavy-metal-contaminants|1]]<span class="cite-card"><span class="cite-card-head"><span class="cite-card-authors">World Health Organization · Administration · 2022</span><a class="cite-card-title" href="../sources/fda2022-tds-elements-fy2018-fy2020">FY2018-FY2020 TDS Elements Analytical Results</a></span><span class="cite-card-body"><span 7 November 2026: Evidence Fill HMI-TOPIC-0206. Added 5 plant-uptake pathway sources (Hashmat 2026, Yang 2025, Faryad 2026, Mesci 2025, Xiong 2026) covering cadmium, chromium, nickel, lead, and copper uptake in Pisum sativum. Filled "How metals reach frozen peas" and "Mitigation for frozen peas" sections. Sources updated from 4 to 9.
